\displaystyle\geq\max_{F\sim\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}u(A^{t},F)-\epsilon

(5)

\displaystyle=\max_{F\sim\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{E}_{A\sim\bar{A}}[u(A,F)]-\epsilon

(6)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{A^{\prime}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})}\max_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A^{\prime}}[u(A,F)]-\epsilon

(7)

where the first inequality follows from the fact that
\bar{F^{\prime}}
is a feasible randomization, the first equality from writing out the expectation explicitly, the second inequality from the fact that point-wise minimizing each term will perform better for the algorithm, the second equality from noting that at each iteration
A^{t}
was a best response to
(F^{\prime})^{t}
, the third inequality from the condition that the regret of Player 2 is at most
\epsilon
, the third equality from writing down the expectation explicitly and the fourth inequality from observing that
\bar{A}
is a feasible randomized algorithm. Comparing equation
(6)
with the LHS of equation
(1)
shows that
\bar{A}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Minmax strategy. Similarly, comparing equation
(1)
with equation
(7)
shows that
\bar{F^{\prime}}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Maxmin strategy.

To show that
(\bar{A},\bar{F^{\prime}})
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Nash equilibrium. Consider the following chain of inequalities.

\mathbf{E}_{A\sim\bar{A}}[\mathbf{E}_{F\sim\bar{F^{\prime}}}[u(A,F)]]\geq\min_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}_{F\sim\bar{F^{\prime}}}[u(A,F)]\geq\max_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{E}_{A\sim\bar{A}}[u(A,F)]-\epsilon

where the second inequality follows from comparing equation
(1)
and
(6)
. Also consider

\mathbf{E}_{A\sim\bar{A}}[\mathbf{E}_{F\sim\bar{F^{\prime}}}[u(A,F)]]\leq\max_{F\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbf{E}_{A\sim\bar{A}}[u(A,F)]\leq\min_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}_{F\sim\bar{F^{\prime}}}[u(A,F)]+\epsilon

where the second inequality again follows from comparing equation
(1)
and
(6)
. Thus
(\bar{A},\bar{F^{\prime}})
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Nash equilbrium.
∎

B.2
Ellipsoid Method

In this section we show that the Ellipsoid method can be used to compute an approximate Nash equilibrium in a two-player zero-sum game where a player who has uncountably many pure strategies has access to a best response oracle. The problem of finding an equilibrium strategy for each player can be written as a linear program where the set of variables map to a mixed strategy for a player and each constraint corresponds to a pure strategy of the opposing player. Unfortunately, uncountable number of strategies for a player correspond to uncountable number of variables and thus cannot be solved efficiently. However, an equilibrium can still be computed by solving the dual program and considering a reduced game where the set of uncountable strategies is reduced to those that were violated during the run of Ellipsoid algorithm for the dual program. This argument was proved by
Hellerstein et al., 2019
. We provide it here for completeness.

Proof of
4.1
.

Let the setting of the zero-sum game be such that
Player 1
has an uncountably large pure strategy space denoted by
\mathcal{A}
. Keeping convention, the utility of
Player 2
for a strategy profile
(A,F)
, where
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}
, is
u(A,F)
. The linear program which solves for the mixed strategy of
Player 2
in equilibrium is given by

\displaystyle\max_{\left(x_{F}\right)_{F\in\mathcal{F}},z}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}x_{F}u(A,F)\geq z,

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{A},

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}}x_{F}=1,

\displaystyle x_{F}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{F}.

This linear program can be solved using the Ellipsoid method, for a suitably chosen initial point which will be described later, where the separation oracle used during the course of algorithm is the best response oracle of
Player 1
. Let
F_{e}
be some fixed pure strategy of
Player 2
. The linear program to solve for the mixed strategy in equilibrium can be rewritten in the standard form as

\displaystyle\max_{\left(x_{F}\right)_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}},z}

\displaystyle z

(
ADV-LP
)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}[u(A,F_{e})-u(A,F)]+z\leq u(A,F_{e}),

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{A},

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}\leq 1,

\displaystyle x_{F}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}.

Let the variables at iteration
t
of the algorithm be denoted as
\left(x_{F}^{t}\right)_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}},z^{t}
. The oracle best-responds to the mixed strategy of
Player 2
given by the variables of the program to obtain
\left(A^{*}\right)^{t}
. The feasibility of the point is then verified by computing the expected utility at the best response. In the case

\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}^{t}u\left(\left(A^{*}\right)^{t},F\right)+\left(1-\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}^{t}\right)u\left(\left(A^{*}\right)^{t},F_{e}\right)<z^{t},

the point is infeasible and a separating hyperplane is given by the vector

\left([u(\left(A^{*}\right)^{t},F)-u(\left(A^{*}\right)^{t},F_{e})]_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}},-1\right).

In the case

\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}^{t}u\left(\left(A^{*}\right)^{t},F\right)+\left(1-\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}^{t}\right)u\left(\left(A^{*}\right)^{t},F_{e}\right)\geq z^{t},

, the additional constraints we need to check is whether the variables
\left(x_{F}\right)_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}
are non-negative and whether
\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}\leq 1
. If any of the variables is non-negative, the separating hyperplane is the unit vector of dimension
|\mathcal{F}|
with the coordinate corresponding to the violated constraint being
1
, while if the constraint
\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}\leq 1
is violated, the separating hyperplane is the vector
\left(1,1,\dots,1,0\right)
with dimension
|\mathcal{F}|
. The total time taken by the separation oracle is linear in the time taken by the separation oracle and
|\mathcal{F}|
.

While running Ellipsoid to solve the program described above will give us the mixed strategy of
Player 2
in equilibrium, it says nothing about the mixed strategy of
Player 1
. To solve for the mixed strategy of
Player 1
, we would have to solve the dual program of
ADV-LP
given by

\displaystyle\max_{\left(y_{A}\right)_{A\in\mathcal{A}},z}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle-\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}}y_{A}u(A,F)\geq z,

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}}y_{A}=1,

\displaystyle y_{A}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{A}.

This program would not be feasible to solve using Ellipsoid as the number of variables, where a variable corresponds to a pure strategy of
Player 1
, is uncountably large. We now show that considering a restricted strategy space suffices to find an approximate minmax strategy of
Player 1
.

Consider the set of algorithms corresponding to the violated constraints in the run of Ellipsoid method while computing an
\epsilon
approximate solution for the program
ADV-LP
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{v}
be the set of algorithms corresponding to the violated constraints in solving
ADV-LP
and consider the reduced game where the strategy space of
Player 2
is
\mathcal{F}
and the strategy space of
Player 1
is
\mathcal{A}_{v}
. Crucially, observe that the size of the pure strategy space of
Player 1
in the reduced game is polynomial in
\text{poly}\left(|\mathcal{F}|,\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)
The linear program to solve for the mixed strategy of
Player 1
in this reduced game is given by

\displaystyle\max_{\left(y_{A}\right)_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{v}},z}

\displaystyle z

(
ALGR-LP
)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle-\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{v}}y_{A}u(A,F)\geq z,

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{F},

\displaystyle\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{v}}y_{A}=1,

\displaystyle y_{A}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{A}_{v}.

The dual of this program allows us to solve for the mixed strategy of
Player 2
in the reduced game, which when written in the standard form, is given by

\displaystyle\max_{\left(x_{F}\right)_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}},z}

\displaystyle z

(
ADVR-LP
)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}[u(A,F_{e})-u(A,F)]+z\leq u(A,F_{e}),

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{A}_{v},

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}}x_{F}\leq 1,

\displaystyle x_{F}\geq 0,

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{F}\setminus F_{e}.

Crucially, the sequence of points generated during the run of Ellipsoid algorithm to solve
ADVR-LP
is identical to the sequence generated during the run of Ellipsoid to solve
ADV-LP
. A consequence of this is that the variable
z
converges to the same variable value in both programs. Thus, if we solve for an
\epsilon
-approximate maxmin strategy, in the game with strategy spaces
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{F}\right)
, by solving
ADV-LP
, then the difference between the value of the game with pure strategy spaces
\left(\mathcal{A}_{v},\mathcal{F}\right)
and the value of the game with pure strategy spaces
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{F}\right)
is at most
\epsilon
. Solving for an
\epsilon
-approximate minmax strategy in the reduced game, by solving
ALGR-LP
using the Ellipsoid method with a separation oracle that goes over each constraint in a brute-force manner, then implies a
2\epsilon
-approximate minmax strategy in the original game.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs For Section 3

Proof of
Lemma

3.1
.

To prove this, we fix a mixed Nash equilibrium in
SRP-R
and show that neither player has an incentive to deviate when considering their strategy profiles in
SRP
.

Consider the mixed strategy of the algorithm player in this equilibrium. The algorithms in
SRP-R
do not depend on the location of the bad weather days. Thus, any order of a fixed number of good weather days gives the adversary the same payoff. It follows that in
SRP
, the adversary has no incentive to deviate.

Consider the mixed strategy of the adversary player in this equilibrium. The algorithm player only needs to take a decision of when to
stop
. The equilibrium strategy of the algorithm player in
SRP-R
does that optimally. It follows that in
SRP
, the algorithm player has no incentive to deviate.
Since no player wants to strictly deviate from the mixed Nash equilibrium strategy profile of
SRP-R
, the statement follows.
∎

Proof of
3.2
.

We find the mixed strategy of the algorithm player in (
SRP-R
). Consider a mixed strategy for the algorithm player, defined as
\{\beta_{0},\dots,\beta_{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1},\beta_{T}\}
, where
\beta_{l}
is the probability that the algorithm plays pure strategy
A^{l}
. For such a mixed strategy, the expected payoff that the adversary player obtains for pure strategy
F^{k}
is

\mathbf{E}[u(A^{l},F^{k})]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{k-1}\beta_{m}\frac{B+m}{k}+\sum\limits_{m=k}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}+\beta_{T}&&\mbox{if }1\leq k\leq T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1\\
\sum\limits_{m=0}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}\frac{B+m}{k}+\beta_{T}&&\mbox{if }T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor\leq k\leq\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1\\
\sum\limits_{m=0}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}\frac{B+m}{B}+\frac{T}{B}\beta_{T}&&\mbox{if }k=T\end{array}\right..

Our analysis can be divided into two cases.

•

Case 1
:
\left\lceil B\right\rceil+\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor\geq T+1
. Observe that the set of pure strategies
\{F^{k}:k\in\{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor+1,\dots,\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1\}\}
is weakly dominated by
F^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor}
. Assuming
k\in\{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor,\dots,\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1\}
, for any algorithm
A^{l}
where
l\in\{0,\dots,T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1\}
, the utility to the adversary is
\frac{B+l}{k}
. For algorithm
A^{T}
, the utility to the adversary is
1
. It follows that
F^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor}
weakly dominates, as it gives a weakly higher payoff for every algorithm.
Thus, it is without loss to further reduce the pure strategy space of the adversary to

\mathcal{F}_{g}=\{F^{k}:k\in\{1,\dots,T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor\}\cup\{T\}\}.

We now solve for mixed Nash equilibrium strategies by assuming that the adversary player puts non-zero mass on every pure strategy in the set described above. By assumption, the expected utility for every pure strategy in
\mathcal{F}_{g}
is equal. We solve this system of equations by induction, considering a pair of expected utility equations at each stage, to derive values for
\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\}
in terms of
\beta_{0}
. For the base case, consider the expected utility equations corresponding to strategy
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. It follows from equating the two that

\displaystyle\beta_{0}B+\sum_{m=1}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}+\beta_{T}

\displaystyle=\beta_{0}\frac{B}{2}+\beta_{1}\frac{B+1}{2}+\sum_{m=2}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}+\beta_{T}

\displaystyle\iff\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{B}{B-1}\beta_{0}.

Assume that for
l\in\{1,2,\dots,T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-2\}
,

\beta_{l}=\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{l}\beta_{0}.

To prove the induction hypothesis for stage
(l+1)
, consider the expected utility equations for strategies
F_{l+1}
and
F_{l+2}
. Equating them, we have that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{m=0}^{l+1}\beta_{m}\frac{B+m}{l+2}+\sum\limits_{m=l+2}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}+\beta_{T}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{l}\beta_{m}\frac{B+m}{l+1}+\sum\limits_{m=l+1}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}+\beta_{T},

\displaystyle\iff\left(\frac{B+l+1}{l+2}-1\right)\beta_{l+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{l}(B+m)\left(\frac{1}{l+1}-\frac{1}{l+2}\right)\beta_{m},

\displaystyle\iff(l+1)(B-1)\beta_{l+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{l}(B+m)\beta_{m}

\displaystyle\iff(l+1)(B-1)\beta_{l+1}

\displaystyle=\beta_{0}\sum_{m=0}^{l}(B+m)\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{m},

\displaystyle\iff\beta_{l+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{l+1}\beta_{0}.

We now solve a pair of simultaneous equations in
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{T}
to arrive at a closed form expression for the mixed Nash equilibrium. First, we must have that

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}+\beta_{T}

\displaystyle=1,

(1)

\displaystyle\iff(B-1)\left[\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor}-1\right]\beta_{0}+\beta_{T}

\displaystyle=1,

where the first equality follows from the fact that we are considering a mixed strategy of the algorithm player. The the second equality follows from the relation between
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{l}
, for
l\in\{1,\dots,T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1\}
, derived above. We obtain a second equation by equating the expected utility function of strategy
F_{1}
and
F_{T}
. It holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}\frac{B+m}{B}+\frac{T}{B}\beta_{T}

\displaystyle=B\beta_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{m}+\beta_{T},

(2)

\displaystyle\iff\beta_{0}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\frac{B+m}{B}\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{m}\right)+\frac{T}{B}\beta_{T}

\displaystyle=B\beta_{0}+\beta_{0}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{m}\right)+\beta_{T},

\displaystyle\iff\frac{T-B}{B}\beta_{T}

\displaystyle=(B+\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-T)\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}\beta_{0}.

Combining
Equation 1
and
Equation 2
, we have that

\beta_{0}=\frac{1}{\frac{B\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor}{T-B}\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1}-(B-1)}.

The result follows by replacing
\beta_{0}
in
Equation 2
to obtain
\beta_{T}
.

•

Case 2
:
\left\lceil B\right\rceil+\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor<T+1
. We follow the same calculation as Case 1 to arrive at an equilibrium strategy. Observe that the set of strategies
\{A^{l}:l\in\{\left\lceil B\right\rceil,\dots,T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1\}\cup\{T\}\}
are weakly dominated by
A^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}
. Assuming
l\geq\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1
, for any
F^{k}
where
k\leq\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1
, the utility to the adversary is
1
. For input
F^{T}
, the utility to the adversary for
A^{l}
, where
l\in\{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1,\dots,T-\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor-1\}
, is
\frac{B+l}{B}
and for
A^{T}
is
\frac{T}{B}
. By assumption that
\left\lceil B\right\rceil+\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor<T+1
, it follows that

\frac{\left\lceil B\right\rceil+B-1}{B}<\frac{\left\lceil B\right\rceil+\left\lfloor B\right\rfloor}{B}\leq\frac{T}{B}.

Thus,
A^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}
weakly dominates
A^{l}
for any
l\geq\left\lceil B\right\rceil
. Consider a mixed strategy of the algorithm player defined as
\{\beta_{0},\dots,\beta_{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}\}
. By following the same calculation as was done in Case 1, assuming that the adversary player puts mass on every pure strategy in equilibrium, we have that

\beta_{l}=\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{l}\beta_{0},\quad\forall l\in\{1,\dots,\left\lceil B\right\rceil-2\}.

To solve for
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}
, we first have

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-2}\beta_{m}+\beta_{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}

\displaystyle=1,

(3)

\displaystyle\iff(B-1)\left[\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}-1\right]\beta_{0}+\beta_{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}

\displaystyle=1,

where the first equality follows from the fact that we are considering a mixed strategy of the algorithm player. By assumption of the adversary mixed strategy having full support, we equate the expected utilities for pure strategies
F_{T}
and
F_{1}
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}\beta_{m}\frac{B+m}{B}

\displaystyle=B\beta_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}\beta_{m},

(4)

\displaystyle\iff\sum_{m=0}^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-2}\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{m}\frac{m}{B}\beta_{0}+\frac{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}{B}\beta_{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}

\displaystyle=(B-1)\beta_{0},

\displaystyle\iff\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-2}\left(B+1-\left\lceil B\right\rceil\right)\frac{B}{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}\beta_{0}

\displaystyle=\beta_{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}.

Solving
Equation 3
and
Equation 4
, we have that

\beta_{0}=\frac{1}{\left(B-1\right)\left[\left(\frac{B}{B-1}\right)^{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}\frac{B}{\left\lceil B\right\rceil-1}-1\right]}.

The theorem statement then follows.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.5
.

It is weakly optimal to always
continue
on a bad weather weather day. There are
0
costs incurred for continuing on the initial
l
contiguous bad weather days. There is no contribution to the optimal hindsight cost from these bad weather days. Thus, it is an instance of the finite-horizon problem with horizon length
(T-l)
on the
(l+1)^{\text{st}}
day.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

Conditional on
continuing
until
(X_{1},\dots,X_{i},1)
, the minimization problem to the algorithm player is the same as that of observing a sequence where all the bad weather days arrive first in a contiguous manner followed by good weather days. By
Lemma

3.5
, the probability of
stopping
, conditional on
continuing
for the observed sequence is given by
\eta_{k}^{T-i+k-1}
.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs For Section 4

We present the missing proofs from
section 4
.

Proof of
4.3
.

Consider a strategy
F^{\prime}
of the adversary in the original game. We propose a corresponding strategy
F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
of the adversary in the reduced game such that for any algorithm
A
, their payoffs are
\epsilon
-close to one another. Index the elements in
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
as
k\in[|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}|]
and let the
k^{th}
element in the order be defined as
(F_{\epsilon})_{k}
. Denote the
\zeta\epsilon
-ball of distributions around
F_{\epsilon}
as
B_{\zeta\epsilon}(F_{\epsilon})
. For
k=1
, make the assignment
Pr[F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}=(F_{\epsilon})_{1}]=Pr[F^{\prime}\in B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{1})\cap\mathcal{F}]
. Consider the set
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{F}\setminus B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{1})
. For
k=2
, make the assignment
Pr[F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}=(F_{\epsilon})_{2}]=Pr[F^{\prime}\in B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{2})\cap\mathcal{F}_{1}]
. If the set
B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{2})\cap\mathcal{F}_{1}
is a null set, assign the probability to be zero. Consider the set
\mathcal{F}_{2}=\mathcal{F}_{1}\setminus B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{2})
. For a general
k
, make the assignment
Pr[F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}=(F_{\epsilon})_{k}]=Pr[F^{\prime}\in B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{k})\cap\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]
. If the set
B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{k})\cap\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
is a null set, assign the probability to be zero. For the next iteration, consider the set
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\setminus B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{k})
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
is an
\epsilon
-cover and
F^{\prime}
is a feasible distribution, this process will terminate and
F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
will be a feasible distribution on
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
. We now show that for any algorithm, the payoffs under these two distributions are
O(\epsilon)
-close to one another. For ease of notation, we re-write
u(A,F):=\frac{A(F)}{\text{OPT}_{F}(F)}
. We can write

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}[u(A,F)]&=\int_{F}u(A,F)dF^{\prime},\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}|}\int_{B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{k})\cap\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}u(A,F)dF^{\prime},\end{split}

\begin{split}&\leq\sum_{k=0}^{|\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}|}\left(u(A,(F_{\epsilon})_{k})+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)\int_{B_{\zeta\epsilon}((F_{\epsilon})_{k})\cap\mathcal{F}_{k-1}}dF^{\prime},\\
&=\mathbf{E}_{F_{\epsilon}\sim F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}[u(A,F_{\epsilon})]+\frac{\epsilon}{4},\end{split}

where the first equality follows from the definition of the expectation, the second equality from the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}
is an
\zeta\epsilon
-cover and the sets that are the limits of the integral are disjoint, the first inequality from
1
. The same calculation can be done for the lower bound i.e.,

\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}[u(A,F)]\geq\mathbf{E}_{F_{\epsilon}\sim F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}[u(A,F_{\epsilon})]-\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

∎

Proof of
4.4
.

Consider an
\frac{\epsilon}{4}
-approximate Nash equilibrium
(A^{*}_{\epsilon},F^{*}_{\epsilon})
in the reduced game. Consider a best response
F^{\prime}
to
A^{*}_{\epsilon}
in the original game. Consider the corresponding strategy to
F^{\prime}
in the reduced game as generated in
4.3
and denote it as
F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
. Since
(A^{*}_{\epsilon},F^{*}_{\epsilon})
were an
\epsilon
-Nash equilibrium,

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A^{*}_{\epsilon}}[\mathbf{E}_{F_{\epsilon}\sim F^{*}_{\epsilon}}[u(A,F_{\epsilon})]]&\geq\max_{F_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{F}_{\epsilon}}\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A^{*}_{\epsilon}}[u(A,F_{\epsilon})]-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\\
&\geq\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A^{*}_{\epsilon}}[\mathbf{E}_{F_{\epsilon}\sim F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}[u(A,F_{\epsilon})]-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\\
&\geq\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A^{*}_{\epsilon}}[\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}[u(A,F)]-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\\
\end{split}

where the first inequality follows from the fact that
(A^{*}_{\epsilon},F^{*}_{\epsilon})
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Nash equilibrium in the reduced game. The second inequality from noting that
F_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
is a feasible distribution in the reduced game. The third equality follows from
4.3
. It follows that
(A^{*}_{\epsilon},F^{*}_{\epsilon})
is an
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
-approximate Nash equilibrium in the original game as
F^{\prime}
is a best response to
A^{*}_{\epsilon}
in the original game and the algorithm has no incentive to
\epsilon
-deviate as its own strategy space was not restricted i.e.,

\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A^{*}_{\epsilon}}[\mathbf{E}_{F_{\epsilon}\sim F^{*}_{\epsilon}}[u(A,F_{\epsilon})]]\leq\min_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}_{F_{\epsilon}\sim F^{*}_{\epsilon}}[u(A,F_{\epsilon})]+\frac{\epsilon}{4}

which follows from the definition that
(A^{*}_{\epsilon},F^{*}_{\epsilon})
was an
\frac{\epsilon}{4}
-Nash equilibrium.
∎

Proof of
4.5
.

Let
\left(A_{r}^{\prime},F^{\prime}\right)
be an
\epsilon
-approximate mixed Nash equilibrium in the reduced game. By definition of an approximate Nash equilibrium, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A_{r}^{\prime}}\left[u\left(A,F\right)\right]\right]\geq\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}\left[u\left(A_{r},F\right)\right]-\epsilon,\quad\forall A_{r}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}.

(5)

Let there exist a pure strategy in
\mathcal{A}
which gives player 1 an
\epsilon
-incentive to deviate, i.e., for some
A\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}\left[u\left(A,F\right)\right]>\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A_{r}^{\prime}}\left[u\left(A,F\right)\right]\right]+\epsilon.

Since there exists a pure strategy in the original game which provides an
\epsilon
-incentive to deviate, any best response to
F^{\prime}
in the original game will also provide player 1 an
\epsilon
-incentive to deviate. By assumption that for any mixed strategy that player
2
chooses, a best response of player
1
lies in a subset of strategies
\mathcal{A}^{*}
, there exists
A_{r}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}\left[u\left(A_{r},F\right)\right]>\mathbf{E}_{F\sim F^{\prime}}\left[\mathbf{E}_{A\sim A_{r}^{\prime}}\left[u\left(A,F\right)\right]\right]+\epsilon.

This contradicts
Equation 5
. Thus, player 1 does not have
\epsilon
-incentive to deviate in the original game.

Player 2 does not have
\epsilon
-incentive to deviate either. This is because we did not restrict the strategy space of player 2 while considering the reduced game. The statement follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 4.6
.

For
p\in\left(\frac{B-1}{T-k},\frac{B-1}{T-k-1}\right]
, where
k\in[\min\{\left\lceil T-B\right\rceil,T-1\}]
, the algorithm will always continue on seeing a good day for all days
i\geq(k+1)
. This is because at the last day, the algorithm always continues, no matter if the weather was good or bad. Let the agent continue on seeing good days at time indices
i\geq i^{\prime}
, where
i^{\prime}\geq(k+2)
. Consider the decision the algorithm makes at time
i^{\prime}-1
on seeing a good day. The agent will also continue as the stopping cost is strictly greater than the continuation costs, i.e
B\geq 1+p(T-k-1)>1+p(T-i^{\prime}+1)
, where
i^{\prime}\geq(k+2)
.

Consider the decision the algorithm makes on seeing a good day at time index
k
. The cost of stopping is
B
, while the cost of continuing is
1+p(T-k)
. Since,
p>\frac{B-1}{T-k}
, we have that the agent will stop on seeing a good day at time index
k
. The optimal algorithm also stops, when it observes a good day, for
i\leq k
. We prove this by induction. The base case is already argued for above. Let the algorithm stop at time index
k^{\prime}\leq k
on seeing a good day. Consider the decision at time
(k^{\prime}-1)
. The cost from stopping is
B
. The cost, if it were to continue can be written as

\begin{split}c^{p}_{k^{\prime}-1}(1)&=1+pB+(1-p)c^{p}_{k^{\prime}}(0)\\
&=1+pB+(1-p)[pc^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(1)+(1-p)c^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(0)]\end{split}

where the second equality follows the optimal algorithm always continuing on seeing bad weather. As the algorithm stopped on seeing a good day at time index
k^{\prime}
, it must have been that
B<1+pc^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(1)+(1-p)c^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(0)
. But then this implies that the agent must stop at time
k^{\prime}-1
when it sees a good day as

\begin{split}B&<1+pc^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(1)+(1-p)c^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(0)\\
&<\frac{1}{1-p}+pc^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(1)+(1-p)c^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(0)\end{split}

and thus
B<1+pB+(1-p)[pc^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(1)+(1-p)c^{p}_{k^{\prime}+1}(0)]
.
For
p\leq\frac{B-1}{T-1}
, the agent will always continue as at any time index the cost of stopping is greater than the cost of continuing given that the algorithm always continues at greater time indices.
∎

We now give a proof for
4.8
.

Proof of
4.8
.

For a distribution over probabilities
P
, an optimal algorithm is obtained by backwards induction. The sketch of the proof is to use induction and then use the fact that for the class of distributions we consider the Bayesian updated distribution will be the same for a same count of good days in any sub-sequence of days.

Observe that the agent never stops on seeing a bad day of weather and thus the property holds whenever the agent observes
X_{i}=0
for all
i\in[T]
. Now consider the case the agent observes
X_{i}=1
. Note that at the last time-step, no matter what the sequence of days observed, the agent will always continue i.e.

A_{T}(X_{1},\dots,X_{T})=C\quad\forall(X_{1},\dots,X_{T})\in\{0,1\}^{T}

This is because of our normalization that the stopping cost
B
is strictly greater than the continuation cost of one. Thus the information-symmetry property is satisfied for functions at stage
T
. Let this property be satisfied by functions above stage
(i+1)
. At stage
i
, for a particular sub-sequence of days
(X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1},1)
, backwards induction will make a comparison between the cost of stopping the sequence and the cost of continuing. The agent will Bayesian update given the knowledge of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{i-1},1)
. Observe that for sub-sequences such that
\sum_{l=1}^{i}X_{l}=k
for any
k\in\{0\}\cup[i-1]
, the continuation cost is the same because the decisions taken in stages
(i+1)
and further on are the same due to the information-symmetry property of functions above stage
(i+1)
. By the assumption on our class of distributions, we have that the Bayesian updated distribution will also be the same given sub-sequences of days with the same count of good days. Hence the same decision will be taken by the backwards induction algorithm and thus information-symmetry property is satisfied by functions at stage
i
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 4.9
.

For a fixed distribution, an optimal algorithm can be found by backwards induction. Recall that
4.8
allows us to focus on those algorithms which are information-symmetric i.e we can only focus on the count of the number of goods for a sub-sequence of a particular length and those which only make stop/continue decisions on observing a good day of weather. Let us denote a sub-sequence of length
i\in[T]
, by
\textbf{X}^{i}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{i})
. Let us denote the continuation cost, as a function of the probability
p\in supp(P)
for a particular distribution
P
, of the algorithm obtained by backwards induction, on seeing a sub-sequence
\textbf{X}^{i}
as
c^{p}(\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}X_{i},i,X_{i})
. Note that for any sub-sequence of length
T
, the agent will continue no matter what i.e. the agent always continues on the last day. This just follows from our normalization that
B>1
and that the cost of continuing is one. Thus we have,

\displaystyle c^{p}(k,T,1)

\displaystyle=1\quad

\displaystyle\forall\quad k\in\{0\}\cup[T-1],\forall\quad p\in supp(P)

\displaystyle c^{p}(k,T,0)

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\forall\quad k\in\{0\}\cup[T-1],\forall\quad p\in supp(P).

For any
\textbf{X}^{T-2}\in\{0,1\}^{T-2}
, to compute the Bayesian updated distribution for a sub-sequence
(\textbf{X}^{T-2},1)
requires
O(|\support(T)|)
operations. Since we have
O(T)
such relevant histories (because we focus only on the count of the number of good days), the total operations required to compute the expected continuation payoffs at the last stage are
O(|\support(T)|^{2})
. Now consider a sub-sequence of length
i\in[T-1]
. For a particular count of good days
k\in\{0\}\cup[i-1]
, to calculate the decision to be taken, which we denote as
(\text{OPT}_{P})_{i}(k,1)
, we need to compare the cost from stopping to the cost of continuing. We need not consider the costs already incurred up until this point as they are sunk. In the equations below,
Pr[(k,i,1)|p]
denotes the probability of observing a particular sub-sequence of length
i
, where among the first
i-1
days we observe
k
good days and the
i^{th}
day is good.

(\text{OPT}_{P})_{i}(k,1)=arg\,min_{\{S,C\}}\{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}Pr[(k,i,1)|p]Pr[P=p]\frac{B}{\text{OPT}_{p}(p)},\\
\sum_{p\in supp(P)}Pr[(k,i,1)|p]Pr[P=p]\left[\frac{1+pc^{p}(k+1,i+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k+1,i+1,0)}{\text{OPT}_{p}(p)}\right]\}

where the first term is the cost from stopping while the second term is the cost from continuing. In the case
(\text{OPT}_{P})_{i+1}(k,1)=S
, then
c^{p}(k,i,1)=B
, otherwise we have
c^{p}(k,i,1)=1+pc^{p}(k+1,i+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k+1,i+1,0)
. We also need to compute the continuation costs if the weather was a bad day.

c^{p}(k,i,0)=pc^{p}(k,i+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k,i+1,0)

Computing each of these, i.e
\forall k\in\{0\}\cup[i]
, requires
O(|\support(P)|i^{2})
time. Thus an optimal algorithm for a fixed distribution with support size
|\support(P)|
can be found in
O(|\support(P)|T^{3})
.
∎

Proof of
4.10
.

The expected cost of any information symmetric algorithm can be computed by a dynamic program. Let us denote the expected continuation cost from time index
i
, on observing
X_{i}
and on observing a count of
k
good days in the first
(i-1)
indices as
c^{p}(k,i,X_{i})
. We can focus on continuation costs of this form as we are working with information-symmetric algorithms. At the last time step we have that

\displaystyle c^{p}(k,T,1)

\displaystyle=B\cdot\mathbbm{1}[A_{T}(k,1)=S]+\mathbbm{1}[A_{T}(k,1)=C]\quad

\displaystyle\forall\quad k\in\{0\}\cup[T-1]

\displaystyle c^{p}(k,T,0)

\displaystyle=B\cdot\mathbbm{1}[A_{T}(k,0)=S]\quad

\displaystyle\forall\quad k\in\{0\}\cup[T-1]

which follow from the definitions of the game i.e. either the algorithm stops and incurs a cost of
B
or otherwise it continues and suffers a cost of one or zero depending on whether
X_{T}=1
or
X_{T}=0
respectively. At any previous time index
i\in[T-1]
, we would have that
\forall k\in\{0\}\cup[i-1]

\displaystyle c^{p}(k,i,1)

\displaystyle=B\cdot\mathbbm{1}[A_{i}(k,1)=S]+\left(1+pc^{p}(k+1,i+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k+1,i+1,0)\right)\mathbbm{1}[A_{i}(k,1)=C]

\displaystyle c^{p}(k,i,0)

\displaystyle=B\cdot\mathbbm{1}[A_{i}(k,0)=S]+\left(pc^{p}(k,i+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k,i+1,0)\right)\mathbbm{1}[A_{i}(k,1)=C]

The expected cost of the information-symmetric algorithm is then

A(p)=pc^{p}(0,1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(0,1,0)

Such a cost calculation will take time
O(T^{3})
to compute. While this was for
A
, note that
\text{OPT}_{p}
is also information-symmetric and thus its expected cost can be computed in the same manner which will take time
O(T^{3})
. Thus the utility can be computed in
O(T^{3})
.
∎

We now provide missing proofs for the small cover property. We first prove a decomposition property of the Bayesian optimal cost, specifically that it can be split into a linear component and a component which is lower bounded by one for any probability in the range where the Bayesian optimal is relevant.

Proof of
4.11
.

Consider the following two random processes. One where, where a sequence of days, denoted by
\textbf{X}^{\prime}
, is sampled i.i.d with probability
p_{\alpha}
, and independently a second random process where a sequence of days, denoted by
\textbf{X}^{\prime\prime}
, is sampled i.i.d with probability
p_{\beta}
. Consider the random variable
A(\textbf{X}^{\prime})-A(\textbf{X}^{\prime\prime})
, which is nothing but the difference in the algorithms cost on the two sampled sequences. Applying the union bound, these two processes sample the same sequence with probability at least
1-\frac{\epsilon}{T}
, and thus
A(\textbf{X}^{\prime})-A(\textbf{X}^{\prime\prime})=0
with probability at least
1-\frac{\epsilon}{T}
. The maximum cost that any algorithm can suffer on any sequence of sampled days is
(B+T)
, as the worst case is when you continue for all days and stop at the end. Thus we have that,

-(B+T)\frac{\epsilon}{T}\leq A(p_{\alpha})-A(p_{\beta})\leq(B+T)\frac{\epsilon}{T}.

The statement follows.
∎

Lemma D.1
.

For any
k\in[\min\{\left\lceil T-B\right\rceil,T-1\}]
, consider the interval
p\in\left(\frac{B-1}{T-k},\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-k-1},1\right\}\right]
. The Bayesian optimal cost can be decomposed as

\text{OPT}_{p}(p)=p.g_{k}(p),

where
g_{k}(p)\geq 1\quad\forall p\in\left(\frac{B-1}{T-k},\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-k-1},1\right\}\right]
. This also holds for the Bayesian optimal cost in the interval
p\in\left[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}\right]
.

Proof.

First consider the probability interval
p\in\left[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}\right]
. The Bayesian optimal algorithm is to continue for all days no matter what the weather and thus
\text{OPT}_{p}(p)=Tp
. The lemma follows for this interval. Now consider any other interval
p\in\left(\frac{B-1}{T-k},\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-k-1},1\right\}\right]
. The Bayesian optimal cost can be written as

\text{OPT}_{p}(p)=\sum_{\textbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{T}}\text{OPT}_{p}(\textbf{X})p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}(1-p)^{T-\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}.

We overloaded notation to denote the cost suffered by the algorithm on a sequence of days as
\text{OPT}_{p}(\textbf{X})
. We have that
\text{OPT}_{p}
never stops on seeing bad weather and thus
\text{OPT}_{p}(\{0\}^{T})=0
. Thus
\text{OPT}_{p}(p)
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\text{OPT}_{p}(p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\textbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{T}\backslash\{0\}^{T}}\text{OPT}_{p}(\textbf{X})p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}(1-p)^{T-\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}

\displaystyle=p\left[\sum_{\textbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{T}\backslash\{0\}^{T}}\text{OPT}_{p}(\textbf{X})p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}-1}(1-p)^{T-\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=p.g_{k}(p).

Analyzing
g_{k}(p)
, we have that

\begin{split}g_{k}(p)&=\sum_{\textbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{T}\backslash\{0\}^{T}}\text{OPT}_{p}(\textbf{X})p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}-1}(1-p)^{T-\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}},\\
&\geq\sum_{\textbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{T}\backslash\{0\}^{T}}p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}-1}(1-p)^{T-\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}},\\
&=\sum_{l=1}^{T}\binom{T}{l}p^{l-1}(1-p)^{T-l}=\sum_{r=0}^{T-1}\binom{T}{r+1}p^{r}(1-p)^{T-1-r},\\
&=\sum_{r=0}^{T-1}\frac{T}{r+1}\binom{T-1}{r}p^{r}(1-p)^{T-1-r},\\
&\geq\sum_{r=0}^{T-1}\binom{T-1}{r}p^{r}(1-p)^{T-1-r}=1,\end{split}

where the first inequality follows from that along any path with at least one good day, the algorithm will suffer a cost of at least one as either it will continue and suffer a cost of one or stop and suffer a cost of
B>1
.
∎

Observe that the proof did not specifically use the structure of the Bayesian optimal algorithm. The Lemma would hold for any algorithm which does not stop on seeing a bad day of weather. With this, we can now prove
Lemma

4.12
which says that for probabilities that are close to each other, for any algorithm
A
, the utilities
\frac{A(p)}{\text{OPT}_{p}(p)}
are close to each other.

Proof of
Lemma

4.12
.

First consider when
p_{\alpha},p_{\beta}
lie in the interval
\left[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}\right]
. The Bayesian optimal cost in this interval is
\text{OPT}_{p}(p)=Tp
. By assumption, the algorithm does not stop on seeing a bad day of weather and thus
A(\{0\}^{T})=0
. We can then write the utility as

\begin{split}u(A,p)&=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\textbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{T}\backslash\{0\}^{T}}A(\textbf{X})p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}-1}(1-p)^{T-\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}\end{split}

(6)

Observe that in its current form, the utility is not an expectation over a feasible probability distribution and thus the arguments of
4.11
do not pass through as is. We re-write the utility function in a manner such that it can be written as the expectation of a function over a feasible distribution. Our goal is to write the utility in a form such that it is generated by a binomial random variable of length
(T-1)
.

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}_{\textbf{X}^{\prime}\sim\Delta(\{0,1\}^{T-1})}[A^{\prime}(\textbf{X}^{\prime})]&=\sum_{\textbf{X}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{T-1}}A^{\prime}(\textbf{X}^{\prime})p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}X_{i}}(1-p)^{T-1-\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}X_{i}}\end{split}

(7)

The idea is to club terms together in
Equation 6
so as to represent the form above. For example, coefficients for sequences
\{\textbf{X}:\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}=1\}
are clubbed together to give

A^{\prime}(\{0\}^{T-1})=\sum_{\{\textbf{X}:\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}=1\}}A(\textbf{X})

In general, for a
r\in[T-1]
, the number of sequences such that
\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}X_{i}^{\prime}=r
are
\binom{T-1}{r}
in
Equation 7
, while the number of sequences such that
\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}=r+1
are
\binom{T}{r+1}
in
Equation 6
. Thus for each term in
Equation 7
we need to club
\frac{\binom{T}{r+1}}{\binom{T-1}{r}}=\frac{T}{r+1}
terms in
Equation 6
. A process to do this would be, for
r\in\{0\}\cup[T-1]
, for the set sequences
S_{r}=\{(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{T-1}^{\prime}):\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}X_{i}^{\prime}=r\}
, consider the set of sequences
S_{r}^{*}=\{(X_{1},\dots,X_{T}):\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}=r+1\}
. Consider any ordering of the sequences in
S_{r}
and assign the elements indices in
\left[\binom{T-1}{r}\right]
. Consider any ordering of the sequences in
S_{r}^{*}
and assign the elements indices in
\left[\binom{T}{r+1}\right]
. Now for sequence indexed
k
in
S_{r}
, which we specifically label
\mathbf{X^{\prime}}^{k}_{r}
, assign the set of sequences indexed
S_{r}^{*^{k}}=\left\{(k-1)*\left\lceil\frac{T}{r+1}\right\rceil+1,\dots,\max\{k*\left\lceil\frac{T}{r+1}\right\rceil,\binom{T}{r+1}\}\right\}
in
S_{*}^{r}
. If
k
is such that
(k-1)*\left\lceil\frac{T}{r+1}\right\rceil+1>\binom{T}{r+1}
, then
S_{r}^{*^{k}}
is the null set. Now we define the function
A^{\prime}(.)
as

A^{\prime}(\mathbf{X^{\prime}}^{k}_{r})=\sum_{\textbf{X}\in S_{r}^{*^{k}}}A(\textbf{X})

Importantly, note that
A^{\prime}(\mathbf{X^{\prime}}^{k}_{r})\leq\left\lceil\frac{T}{r+1}\right\rceil\max A(\textbf{X})\leq\left\lceil\frac{T}{r+1}\right\rceil(B+r+1)
, where the second inequality holds because the assignment is over paths that observe
(r+1)
good days and thus the maximum cost suffered by any algorithm on this path is at most
(B+r+1)
. With this assignment, we have that

u(A,p)=\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{E}_{\textbf{X}^{\prime}\sim\Delta(\{0,1\}^{T-1})}[A^{\prime}(\textbf{X}^{\prime})]

where the distribution is generated by a binomial process with probability
p
and length
(T-1)
. Applying
4.11
, we have that if
|p_{\alpha}-p_{\beta}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2(B+1)^{2}(T+1)^{2}T}
, when
p_{\alpha},p_{\beta}\in\left[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}\right]
,

|u(A,p_{\alpha})-u(A,p_{\beta})|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2T(B+1)(T+1)}\leq\epsilon

where the second inequality follows from that when we apply
4.11
, the worst case cost along a path is upper bounded as
\max_{r}\left\lceil\frac{T}{r+1}\right\rceil(B+r+1)
which is maximized at
r=0
. Now consider the case
p_{\alpha},p_{\beta}
in the interval
\left(\frac{B-1}{T-k},\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-k-1},1\right\}\right]
such that

|p_{\alpha}-p_{\beta}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2(B+1)^{2}(T+1)^{2}T}

We have that

\begin{split}|u(A,p_{\alpha})-u(A,p_{\beta})|&=\left|\frac{A(p_{\alpha})}{\text{OPT}_{p_{\alpha}}(p_{\alpha})}-\frac{A(p_{\beta})}{\text{OPT}_{p_{\beta}}(p_{\beta})}\right|\\
&=\left|\frac{A^{\prime}(p_{\alpha})}{g_{k}(p_{\alpha})}-\frac{A^{\prime}(p_{\beta})}{g_{k}(p_{\beta})}\right|\\
&\leq\epsilon\end{split}

where the second equality follows from
D.1
. Note that
g_{k}
can also be decomposed in an expected form and thus the Lipschitz bound derived above holds for
g_{k}(p)
as well. Thus the inequality follows from noting that
g_{k}(.)\geq 1
for all
p\in[\delta,1]
(
D.1
), and an upper bound on both
A^{\prime}(.)
and
g_{k}(.)
is
\max_{r}\left\lceil\frac{T}{r+1}\right\rceil(B+r+1)
which is upper bounded by
(B+1)(T+1)
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 4.13
.

The idea is to discretize each interval where the Bayesian optimal algorithm is the same (
Theorem 4.6
), with a discretization of size

\bar{\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon}{8(B+1)^{2}(T+1)^{2}T}.

First consider the interval
\left[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}\right]
, where the Bayesian optimal algorithm always rents. Consider the following grid points on this interval

G_{0}=\left\{\delta+k\bar{\epsilon}\right\}_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{1}{\bar{\epsilon}}\left[\frac{B-1}{T-1}-\delta\right]-1\right\rceil}.

Such a discretization covers the interval
\left[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}\right]
. We have that for any grid point in
G_{0}
i.e.
(p_{\epsilon})_{k}\in G_{0}
,

|u(A,(p_{\epsilon})_{k})-u(A,p)|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\quad\forall p\in\left[(p_{\epsilon})_{k}-\bar{\epsilon},(p_{\epsilon})_{k}+\bar{\epsilon}\right]

which follows from
Lemma

4.12
. We do this process for every interval
\left[\frac{B-1}{T-l},\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-l-1},1\right\}\right]
, where
l\in[\min\{\left\lceil T-B\right\rceil,T-1\}]
to obtain a grid for that interval
G_{l}
.

G_{l}=\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-l}+k\bar{\epsilon}\right\}_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{1}{\bar{\epsilon}}\left[\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-l-1},1\right\}-\frac{B-1}{T-l}\right]-1\right\rceil}

.

By the same argument for
G_{0}
, these satisfy the same properties as well. Hence

\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon}=\cup_{l=0}^{\min\{\left\lceil T-B\right\rceil,T-1\}}G_{l}

satisfies Small-Cover.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 4.14
.

To compute the run-time, first note that the number of actions to the distribution player is bounded as
|\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon}|\leq\frac{2(B+1)^{2}(T+1)^{2}T}{\epsilon}
(look at proof of
Theorem 4.13
). Also note that the maximum possible utility encountered by the learning algorithm is
(B+1)(T+1)
(look at the proof of
Lemma

4.12
in
Appendix D
). The number of updates the algorithm takes is
\frac{(B+1)^{2}(T+1)^{2}\log|\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon}|}{\epsilon^{2}}
. Each update takes
O(|\mathcal{P}_{\epsilon}|T^{3})
time to execute (
Theorem 4.9
). Thus the overall running time is
\tilde{O}(T^{12}\epsilon^{-3})
where we have used the inequality
B\leq T
as the problem is trivial when
B>T
in which case the agent always continues.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs For Section 5

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

By definition, the sub-game optimal algorithm takes an optimal decision at every stage of the game. Since the worst-case optimal algorithm takes an optimal decision on some input sequence, it follows that the sets have a non-empty intersection.
∎

Appendix F
Properties of the Optimal Prior-Independent Ski-Rental Algorithm

In this section, we describe certain properties of an optimal prior-independent ski-rental algorithm. We first define what we mean by an algorithm to satisfy the thresholding property. Intuitively, for any sub-sequence of days of a fixed length, if the algorithm stops on seeing a certain count of good days, then it will also stop on seeing a greater count of good days. We then show a property of the worst-case distribution that the adversary plays.

Definition 14
.

(Thresholding Algorithm) We say that an algorithm
A=(A_{1},\dots,A_{T})
is a thresholding algorithm if
\forall i\in[T]
,
\forall X_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, if
\exists k\in\{0\}\cup[i-1]
such that
A_{i}(k,X_{i})=S
, then
A_{i}(k+1,X_{i})=S

Lemma F.1
.

For any distribution,
P\in\Delta([\delta,1])
that the adversary plays, an optimal algorithm for that distribution satisfies the thresholding property.

Proof.

We show the above Lemma for any discrete distribution, but the same arguments go through for a continuous distribution as well. We use the same notation as that used in
Theorem 4.9
. We do an induction over the length of sub-sequences
n\in[T]
, while maintaining the following invariant at each stage

•

The function
u_{t}(k)
, where
u_{t}(k)
is given as,

u_{t}(k)=\frac{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}c^{p}(k,t,1)}{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}}

is monotone non-decreasing in
k
.

•

The function
l_{t}(k)
, where
l_{t}(k)
is given as,

l_{t}(k)=\frac{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,0)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}c^{p}(k,t,0)}{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,0)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}}

is monotone non-decreasing in
k
.

•

u_{t}(k)\geq l_{t}(k)
for all
k\in\{0\}\cup[t-1]
.

Let us first check the base case when
t=T
. We always continue on the last day no matter what and thus we have that
c^{p}(k,T,1)=1
and
c^{p}(k,T,0)=0
for all
k\in\{0\}\cup[T-1]
and
p\in supp(P)
. Thus the base case is satisfied. Also observe that thresholding is vacuously satisfied at stage
t=T
. We now prove that when the induction assumptions are satisfied at stage
(n+1)
, then we get the thresholding property of the algorithm at stage
n
. It is sufficient to prove that on observing a good day, the normalized continuation cost of continueing is monotone non-decreasing in
k
. This is because the algorithm takes a decision according to

A^{P}_{t}(k,1)=arg\,min_{\{B,R\}}\{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]\frac{B}{OPT(p)},\\
\sum_{p\in supp(P)}Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]\left[\frac{1+pc^{p}(k+1,t+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k+1,t+1,0)}{OPT(p)}\right]\}

or effectively compares the stop cost
B
against

u^{R}_{t}(k)=\frac{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}\left[1+pc^{p}(k+1,t+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k+1,t+1,0)\right]}{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}}

Thus if the latter is monotone non-decreasing, if at any
k
it becomes greater than
B
, it will still be greater than
B
for larger
k
. We ignore writing the limits of the summation as it is clear it is over probabilites in the support. To make the calculations more compact, we re-write the weighted probability as

f^{p}(k,t,X_{T})=\frac{Pr[(k,t,X_{T})|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)},\quad X_{T}\in\{0,1\}

Our aim is to prove
u^{R}_{t}(k+1)-u^{R}_{t}(k)\geq 0
. We have

u^{R}_{t}(k+1)-u^{R}_{t}(k)=\overbrace{\sum p\left[\frac{f^{p}(k+1,t,1)}{\sum f^{p}(k+1,t,1)}c^{p}(k+2,t+1,1)-\frac{f^{p}(k,t,1)}{\sum f^{p}(k,t,1)}c^{p}(k+1,t+1,1)\right]}^{\mathchoice{\hbox to8.1pt{\vbox to8.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.05107pt\lower-1.79552pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.33 3.12 C 5.33 6.06 2.94 8.45 0 8.45 C -2.94 8.45 -5.33 6.06 -5.33 3.12 C -5.33 0.18 -2.94 -2.21 0 -2.21 C 2.94 -2.21 5.33 0.18 5.33 3.12 Z M 0 3.12}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Utilizing the inductive assumption corresponding to the non-decreasing property of

Thus combining
\textstyle 1
and
\textstyle 2
to give a bound on
u^{R}_{t}(k+1)-u^{R}_{t}(k)
, we have

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.64583pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\left[\sum\frac{f^{p}(k+1,t+1,1)c^{p}(k+1,t+1,1)}{\sum f^{p}(k+1,t+1,1)}-\sum\frac{f^{p}(k+1,t+1,0)c^{p}(k+1,t+1,0)}{\sum f^{p}(k+1,t+1,0)}\right]\\
&\geq 0\end{split}

which follows from
\textstyle 1b
being non-negative and the inductive assumption that
u_{t+1}(k)\geq l_{t+1}(k)
.

All that is left to verify is that the inductive invariants are maintained at stage
t
. Since the algorithm is thresholding at stage
t
, let the algorithm continue till a count of
k^{*}
and then stop for strictly greater than
k^{*}
good days. If
k^{*}=(t-1)
, then the inductive invariant holds as
u_{t}(k)=u^{R}_{t}(k)
for all
k\in\{0\}\cup[t-1]
, which we have proved to be monotonic non-decreasing in
k
. If
k^{*}<t
, then
u_{t}(k)
is monotone non-decreasing for
k\in\{0\}\cup[k^{*}]
. We also have that
u_{t}(k)
is monotone non-decreasing for
k\in\{k^{*}+1,t-1\}
as
c^{p}(k,t,1)=B
for all
p\in supp(P)
. All that is remaining to show is that
u_{t}(k^{*}+1)\geq u_{t}(k^{*})
.

u_{t}(k^{*}+1)=\frac{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k^{*}+1,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}B}{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k^{*}+1,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}}=B>u_{t}^{R}(k^{*})=u_{t}(k^{*})

where the inequality follows from the fact that the algorithm chose to continue on seeing
k^{*}
good days in the first
(t-1)
days. Now to verify the monotone non-decreasing nature of
l_{t}(k)
, we follows the same procedure as that done to show the monotonicity of
u_{t}^{R}(k)
. Note that the algorithm always continues on seeing a bad day of weather. Thus,

l_{t}(k+1)-l_{t}(k)=\overbrace{\sum p\left[\frac{f^{p}(k+1,t,0)}{\sum f^{p}(k+1,t,0)}c^{p}(k+1,t+1,1)-\frac{f^{p}(k,t,0)}{\sum f^{p}(k,t,0)}c^{p}(k,t+1,1)\right]}^{\mathchoice{\hbox to8.1pt{\vbox to8.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.05107pt\lower-1.79552pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.33 3.12 C 5.33 6.06 2.94 8.45 0 8.45 C -2.94 8.45 -5.33 6.06 -5.33 3.12 C -5.33 0.18 -2.94 -2.21 0 -2.21 C 2.94 -2.21 5.33 0.18 5.33 3.12 Z M 0 3.12}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.99306pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

&\geq-\mathchoice{\hbox to14.43pt{\vbox to14.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.21367pt\lower-3.74146pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.7 4.8 C 9.7 10.16 5.36 14.51 0 14.51 C -5.36 14.51 -9.7 10.16 -9.7 4.8 C -9.7 -0.56 -5.36 -4.9 0 -4.9 C 5.36 -4.9 9.7 -0.56 9.7 4.8 Z M 0 4.8}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Thus combining
\textstyle 3
and
\textstyle 4
to give a bound on
l_{t}(k+1)-l_{t}(k)
, we have

\begin{split}l_{t}(k+1)-l_{t}(k)&\geq\mathchoice{\hbox to14.43pt{\vbox to14.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.21367pt\lower-3.74146pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.7 4.8 C 9.7 10.16 5.36 14.51 0 14.51 C -5.36 14.51 -9.7 10.16 -9.7 4.8 C -9.7 -0.56 -5.36 -4.9 0 -4.9 C 5.36 -4.9 9.7 -0.56 9.7 4.8 Z M 0 4.8}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.64583pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.64583pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\hbox to10.18pt{\vbox to10.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-3.35477pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.77 2.4 C 6.77 6.14 3.74 9.17 0 9.17 C -3.74 9.17 -6.77 6.14 -6.77 2.4 C -6.77 -1.34 -3.74 -4.37 0 -4.37 C 3.74 -4.37 6.77 -1.34 6.77 2.4 Z M 0 2.4}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.28474pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{68}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\left[\sum\frac{f^{p}(k,t+1,1)c^{p}(k,t+1,1)}{\sum f^{p}(k,t+1,1)}-\sum\frac{f^{p}(k,t+1,0)c^{p}(k,t+1,0)}{\sum f^{p}(k,t+1,0)}\right]\\
&\geq 0\end{split}

which follows from
\textstyle 3b
being non-negative and the inductive assumption that
u_{t+1}(k)\geq l_{t+1}(k)
.

All that is left to prove that the third induction assumption is maintained i.e
u_{t}(k)\geq l_{t}(k)\forall k\in\{0\}\cup[t-1]
. First note that if at some
k^{*}\in\{0\}\cup[t-1]
, the algorithm stops, then we clearly have for all
k\geq k^{*}
,

u_{t}(k)=\frac{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}B}{\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,1)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}}=B>l_{t}(k)

where the first equality follows from the fact that
c^{p}(k,t,1)=B\quad\forall p\in supp(P),\forall k\geq k^{*}
and the inequality from the fact that the agent never stops on seeing a bad day in the Backwards Induction algorithm and thus it must have been that

\begin{split}B\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,0)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}&>\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,0)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}[pc^{p}(k,t+1,1)+(1-p)c^{p}(k,t+1,0)]\\
&=\sum_{p\in supp(P)}\frac{Pr[(k,t,0)|p]Pr[P=p]}{OPT(p)}c^{p}(k,t,0)\end{split}

For
k<k^{*}
, i.e those count of days where the agent continues, we have that

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\geq 0
and the inductive assumption
u_{t+1}(k)\geq l_{t+1}(k)
at stage
(t+1)
.
∎

With these individual properties, we can now describe a property of the optimal prior-independent algorithm.

Corollary F.2
.

There exists an optimal prior-independent algorithm such that all deterministic algorithms in its support are information-symmetric, do not stop on seeing bad weather and satisfy the thresholding property.

Proof.

Observe that Backwards Induction gave us an algorithm in which two properties were satisfied,
4.8
and
F.1
. For any mixed strategy that the adversary plays, a best response of the agent is satisfies the above three properties. Applying
4.5
then leads to the Corollary.
∎

F.2
tells us that we can work with a reduced game where strategies of the algorithm satisfy the described three properties. We can then show a property of the worst-case distribution of the adversary in equilibrium.

Lemma F.3
.

For any given
T
, for a fixed strategy of the agent (an algorithm) which does not stop on seeing bad weather, any best response of the adversary is a discrete distribution.

Proof.

4.7
tells us that for a deterministic algorithm
A
, and a fixed distribution
p\in[\delta,1]
, the cost suffered by the algorithm
A(p)
is a polynomial in
p
with degree at most
T
. This would also hold for randomized algorithms as they are nothing but a convex combination over deterministic algorithms. Recall that the expected cost of the algorithm for a distribution
p
, is

A(p)=\sum_{\textbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{T}}A(\textbf{X})p^{\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}(1-p)^{T-\sum_{i=1}^{T}X_{i}}

where
A(\textbf{X})
is the cost of the algorithm along a sampled sequence
\textbf{X}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{T})
.

First note that since we are in a setting where the class of algorithms are those which do not stop on seeing a bad day of weather, the cost of an algorithm cannot be a constant as a function of
p
. This is because
A(\{0,\dots,0\})=0
. The cost of any algorithm is a polynomial of degree at least one.

Let the algorithms cost be of degree one, that is,
A(p)=\alpha p
for some
\alpha>0
. Now for
p\in[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}]
, the utility of the adversary is
\frac{\alpha}{T}
, while for
p\in(\frac{B-1}{T-1},\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-2},1\right\}]
, the utility of the adversary is
\frac{\alpha}{B+(1-p)(T-1)}>\frac{\alpha}{T}
, where the utility was obtained by replacing
\text{OPT}_{p}(p)
. Observe that the utility is strictly increasing in
p
and thus is maximized at
\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-2},1\right\}
. For each interval
p\in\left(\frac{B-1}{T-k},\min\left\{\frac{B-1}{T-k-1},1\right\}\right]
, where
k\in[\min\{\left\lceil T-B\right\rceil,T-1\}]
, the utility is a ratio of two polynomials in
p
of degree at most
T
. Thus the number of local extrema of the utility function in each interval are
O(T)
, and thus the number of global maximizers in each interval are
O(T)
. Since a necessary condition of a best response is that the probabilities in the support must be global maximizers in each interval, we have that any best response has at most
O(T^{2})
probabilities in its support, as there are
O(T)
intervals.

Let the algorithms cost be of degree at least two. In that case, the utility in the interval
p\in[\delta,\frac{B-1}{T-1}]
is no longer a constant but of degree at least one. The same argument of
O(T)
local maximizers in each interval follows and we have that any best response has at most
O(T^{2})
probabilities in its support.
∎

Corollary F.4
.

For a given
T
, the worst-case distribution in the prior-independent ski-rental problem is a discrete distribution.
